ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e4519	Ladies and gentlemen, we have really discussed this very thoroughly in the Committee on Agriculture. The protected geographical indication, p.a., is clearly regulated in EU Regulation 1151/2012 on quality schemes for agricultural products and foodstuffs. The two different categories of designations of origin, designations of origin and geographical indications correspond to the practices in the Member States and have therefore been incorporated into the EU Regulation. In geographical terms, the relationship is often based on the reputation of this name, i.e. a product from a particular area, the keyword of Tyrolean bacon, is therefore asked because it has a special reputation for consumers or because there is a special form of production behind it. However, this reputation does not necessarily derive from the origin of raw materials, which I have repeatedly clarified. A raw material is refined by the special form of production, and thus this designation of origin is also permitted and correct.

ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e4801	Federal Minister, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e4817	Mr Höfinger, it is indeed so that we can celebrate 20 years of genetic engineering freedom in Austria. The genetic engineering people's desire was indeed the second most successful popular desire in Austria, and it was 20 years ago that the population's desire was maintained. Since then, we have always implemented the relevant legal regulations in an all-party consensus in Austria. At EU level, detailed legal provisions were still missing, which were only gradually created in early 2000 and 2003 with Council and European Parliament regulations. This was about authorisation procedures, traceability, labelling and registration of genetically modified organisms; in the meantime, GMOs are also authorised in the EU. We have always insisted on being able to determine for ourselves whether we want to allow the use of GMOs in agriculture or not, and we have been able to enforce them. In the 2014 Environment Council, for example – that was the first time I was there – we were actually able to achieve a breakthrough in the way we did this self- the right of establishment of the Member States. On this basis, we have here in the House of Lords this ban on the use of genetically modified organisms in cultivation, I believe, even enforced unanimously, and it works and is implemented.

These are things that one should not do in times of moving economics, because that is putting money out of the window! What should we do? . We should create incentives instead of promoting the casting pot, we should free the economy from the many shackles there to make the economy work in times of rising economic activity. That is why it is also necessary to reduce bureaucracy, to highlight the regulatory jungle. The trade regulation was also an issue, but unfortunately this is not going to happen again today – a proper trade reform would be just like a measure to free the economy. It would be necessary to simplify tax law, simplify the wage settlement, which is incredibly complex, and reduce taxes. Mr Schellhorn has already said: Very simple measures, such as shortening depreciation time, have investment-promoting effects. – These are things that would cost nothing and bring a lot. That's what we're for. In any case, we are one of the following: for the principle of self-reliance, for positive access to competition, but also for positive access to solidarity. We are a social state, we are responsible for Austrian citizens – we are here for that! We are in favour of creating a social network, we are proud of it, but we are not in favour of creating a social hammock.

ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e12146	Mr President. Minister! In addition to the rules of commerce, we decide on other laws, including the law on measures and eich. We create a clear administrative simplification with the novice of the law of measurement and eich, and this also entails a noticeable cost savings in the field of eich and measuring equipment. In the sense of de-bureaucratisation, facilitation and deregulation, the provisions of the Eich Act have been examined and no longer necessary regulations and provisions have been deleted. Due to the continuous development in the technical field, it is now possible to reduce even contingency obligations and to extend the intervals for the verification of the equipment. Based on this finding, ladies and gentlemen, a series of measuring instruments, such as the wastewater meters or the speedometers, are being removed. For other devices, such as the length measuring instruments, the obligation to re-establish is not met, whereas the extension of the subsequent periods also affects electronic electricity meters, gas meters, fuel taps or tyre pressure meters. At present, some 700 000 measuring instruments are issued per year in Austria, and with full effectiveness, some 167 000 of these 700 000 equivalents are lost each year. In most cases, the removal of consignee obligations or the extension of post-examination periods will be effective with the entry into force of the novelle, only the extension of the post-examination periods will occur for a few devices with a corresponding delay. Thus, there is a clear relief for users and users of measuring instruments. This also leads to a sustainable strengthening of the economic location and competitiveness. Of course, the interests of consumers are also safeguarded, and the provisions on health and safety are clearly being fulfilled.

Austria is rich in such varieties, and we must preserve this abundance. Regionality is the economic concept of the future for Austria and must also be it; it is also the guarantee of sustainable management of genetic resources. The amendment to the Plant Goods Act in this form includes adaptations in accordance with some implementing directives of the European Union. The essential contents of the novels are the implementation of the relevant directives. In order to improve quality, there are more detailed regulations on higher-quality material, there is a clarification of enforcement rules, the safeguarding and approval of traditional varieties. With regard to the registration of fruit plant material, for example, the list of harmful organisms will be amended. In principle, a common list of varieties is also provided for by the Federal Office for Wine and Fruit and Food Safety. Old fruit varieties – we need to be careful that we can preserve them – make an important contribution to biodiversity, and this is certainly a success story of local agriculture. The OPUL programme also makes a significant contribution to the preservation of these varieties. As far as implementation is concerned, there will be only a small effort or extra effort for the around one hundred companies concerned. A cost increase is not to be expected from farmers because the total cost to authorities will be € 10,000. In the sense of domestic agriculture, quality assurance and improvement are being promoted. The awareness of old varieties, as I have already mentioned, should be strengthened even more, it should be strengthened. But overall, Mr Federal Minister, we are on a good path, and I would like to thank you very much for this, in the sense of sustainable agriculture, in the sense of our farmers, but also in the sense of our consumers, who place particular emphasis on quality production and on their own supply.

This is true, and the directives we are implementing here take account of this. In Austria, however, the concern is always that old, traditional farm varieties are at risk, in the case of fruits, vegetables, cereals, but also pet breeds. Austria has always interpreted this scheme as small-scale, most friendly of all EU countries. This was also the case at the time when in 2010 the European Union wanted to make a seed regulation and many small producers were concerned that they had to pay a lot of money for approval and then the small producers and these old, traditional agricultural varieties disappeared. At the time – I was allowed to be Minister of Agriculture at the time – we started a seed dialogue in the Ministry of Seeds, in order to safeguard the domestic seed industry and on the other hand to give these small producers prospects so that they are not overrun by the supranational seed groups. The effect was successful in so far as the EU withdrew the Seed Regulation and has now made directives in a weakened form, which apply only to breeding species. The traditional agricultural varieties are excluded, there is no need for a new authorisation, and thus the small producers save themselves a lot of money. In addition, the list of varieties was made by the Federal Office for Wine and Fruit, where technical support is also provided by the school for wine and fruit. That means we want to protect these small producers. This also applies to patents. No patents on plants and animals, even this is a subject that we always discuss very hot and where Austria is ahead. – So far, thank you very much for your attention.

[[Congratulations with the FPÖ as well as with representatives of ÖVP and Team Stronach.]] In the current debate, we are dealing with the government template to amend the 2002 Waste Management Act. The focus of this novelle is on the implementation of the 2012 directive on the control of the risks of serious accidents involving dangerous substances, amending and subsequently repealing the Seveso III Directive. A legal basis for the collection of waste is to be established in the Waste Management Act 2002. The provisions laid down in Article 75b of the draft concerning the confiscation and disposal of waste are intended to prevent illegal shipments of waste. I miss a lot of things, too, and this government proposal or the proposal for a law raises many questions. There are also numerous opinions on this draft, which were addressed to you, and you also had sufficient time to draw it up. This too has unfortunately not happened in this form. One point is also the question of costs and the question of additional expenditure. I refer here to an opinion by the Lower Austrian Government, which fears that the proposed seizure and waste disposal, including transport packaging, will be linked to a significant increase in the cost to the district administration authority. And it also raises the question of what to do with these wastes when they are confiscated. Another criticism comes from the Court of Auditors. It is expected that the seizure of waste will lead to an increase in storage costs. And here, Mr Federal Minister, I do not read from your side anything about how you want to pay off these extra costs financially. I can remember that the Committee has estimated this extra effort with a sum of € 100 000, which is to be sufficient throughout Austria. This figure seems to me somewhat low, or at least insufficient, to be able to implement this measure accordingly. I would like to address one last point, namely the opinion of the Chamber of Commerce. This refers to the fact that in the cases of § 6 para. 1 the competent authority may request a notice from the district administration authority that the Authority has six months to adopt that decision. Here there is a time difference between the confiscation of the good and the determination of the product. There are different opinions, and the Chamber of Commerce criticizes the vehement. Unfortunately, you have not been able to explain the administrative simplification and the financial discharge by the authorities. ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e15941	Next speaker: Mrs Mag. Greiner. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e15963	Madam President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! What is the aim of this novel in the Waste Management Act? – The aim is to identify the possible risks in the event of serious accidents with to exclude dangerous substances if possible and then, if they are given, to be able to deal with them accordingly and to pose hazards and, above all, consequences to health and the environment. I am now taking a closer look at the Seveso III directive. The name of this directive – it has already been briefly heard today – goes back to a chemical accident in the 1970s, which occurred in the small town of Italy. A very serious accident occurred at the time: Dioxin gases and the resulting toxic clouds have caused significant damage to humans, fauna and flora. This has also led to the publication of a first Seveso Directive at the beginning of the 1980s, later a second, and in 2015 an EU directive was added, namely this Seveso III Directive. In order to be able to implement this EU Directive also nationally, several legislative adaptations were necessary, for example in the trade regulations, in the Mineral Oil Raw Materials Act, in the Waste Management Act, in the Emissions Protection Act et cetera. It also works at the national level, as the areas of spatial planning or disaster protection are affected. What is the content of this novel? – Their content is, among other things, that farmers have to take or take measures to prevent serious accidents with hazardous substances or that, if something happens, one can really handle them. It therefore regulates the handling of hazardous substances, for example in cement plants or in waste-incinerators, and also generally regulates the professional handling of chemicals and problem substances. This amendment not only regulates what farmers have to do and what steps they have to take, but also regulates how authorities have to check. Ladies and gentlemen! Today, we decide to implement this Seveso III directive in the Waste Management Act, thereby also implementing the relevant EU standards. I'm looking for you for broad approval. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e16440	We're now on the 9th. Agenda item. There was no oral reporting. As First of all, Mr Magistrate, is speaking. Smoking. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e16467	Madam President. Federal Minister! We discussed this agreement at the Environment Committee last week. [[The President-in-Office of the Council shall take over the Presidency.]] We all know that mercury is a very toxic, so very toxic heavy metal, which can have an impact on the nervous system and can naturally cause chronic and acute poisoning. How does this mercury come about? – In a natural way, for example in the case of volcanic eruptions, but also, and this is very strong, in industrial processes, and this naturally leads to the environment. The methyl mercury represents the most dangerous form. When this enters the sea, fish and seafood enter the food chain. Individual fish species are particularly affected, in which the limit values for mercury values are very often exceeded. This Minamata Convention was adopted to protect the environment and, above all, human health from sustainable damage. In Minamata there was a factory that led mercury to the sea via the wastewater. At that time, more than 2,000 people were killed, and more than ten thousand were poisoned because this poison came across the food chain. Austria was, and this is very gratifying, one of the first countries to have signed this agreement in 2013. At present, there are about 130 states that have signed the agreement, and 43 have already ratified it. 50 ratifications are necessary for the entry into force of this Agreement. As far as I am aware, the EU will deposit these ratifications together with Austria and the other Member States as documents, and this will allow the agreement to enter into force. From my point of view, this is a very important environmental policy agreement, which will significantly restrict the treatment of mercury in the future. There are special rules in place, but in principle it is about limiting the use of mercury, and that is why we are happy to agree to this request today. – Thank you for your attention.
ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e16511	Next speaker: Mr Keck. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e16534	Mr President. Federal Minister! The aim of the Minamata Convention on Mercury is to create an international instrument for the protection of humans and the environment from mercury of any kind. Mercury is an extremely toxic heavy metal, which is massively enriched in humans, animals and the environment. It affects the central nervous system and can cause chronic and acute poisoning. The Minamata Convention is the first global regulatory instrument ever to limit the primary mining of mercury in the future. Furthermore, the production and trade of mercury products, such as batteries, electronic components, soaps, pesticides and measuring instruments, will also be limited. According to a report from the European Environment Agency, burning coal and other fossil fuels worldwide is the main cause of mercury emissions. Other essential sources are the landfill of waste, crematories and the extraction of gold and the production of cement and metal. Especially in metal production, it is also the voestalpine in Linz, which is a global pioneer in reducing and monitoring mercury emissions. We have set environmental standards in this company when cleaning the exhaust gases in the sink system, which are used as an example worldwide. If the standards of voestalpine were to apply worldwide, we probably would not need such an agreement because there would not be these pollutants in this form. It is essential that we also deal with the environment carefully in industry. This also includes a sustainable handling of dangerous chemicals such as mercury, as is done in the voestalpine Linz in practice constantly. While there are already strict rules in the area of mercury emissions within the EU, the Minamata Convention helps to spread the high standards worldwide, I think that is, also, ladies and gentlemen. The objective is thus very clear: to limit the global use of mercury severely, which is expected to result in a medium to long-term reduction in environmental contamination worldwide.

ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e17091	Mr President. Minister! My dear! Ladies and gentlemen! Minamata, the city stands for one of the most serious environmental disasters with mercury that the earth has ever experienced, and therefore it is also good that one has named after this city in memory of this disaster. When mercury reaches the sea, it is very difficult to decompose, because it adheres to the salt, and through the salt it passes through the fish and crustaceans into the food chain and can cause massive damage until malformations. It is therefore important to reduce or completely avoid the use of mercury as much as possible. After 1950, many thousands of people in Minamata suffer from this poisoning, the Minamata disease, and they still suffer from these diseases today. As already reported, the Minamata Agreement was signed by the representatives of Austria in 2013 and is now ratified so that it can be ratified in general in the EU. Yesterday, we spoke of the EU's added value: In environmental policy too, it is important for the EU to insist that all states are invited to ratify this agreement, and that the EU as a whole should point out that it is important to us. With this agreement we want to protect the environment and human health. In mining – this is an important point – this has to be stopped. In the manufacture of products such as batteries, in which it was always contained, it is significantly restricted, but also mercury was present in electronic components, pesticides or soaps. Austria and the EU have a high degree of implementation, and the Austrian Federal Environment Office is also responsible for examining the current implementation accordingly. Thanks also to the pharmacy chamber for the exchange campaign ten years ago, which at that time supported a conversion away from mercury-containing fever thermometers. It is important that we inform the population that the mercury-containing fever thermometers can continue to be returned free of charge at all waste collection sites. For example, today's ratification is not only an important step in international environmental policy, it should also call on Austrian households to return the mercury thermometers so that households become mercury-free. - Thank you.
ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e17131	Next speaker: Mr Schopf. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2017-05-17-025-XXV-NRSITZ-00181_d8e17152	Mr President. Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! In the area of mercury prevention, Austria is a pioneer, especially as regards the Minamata Convention, for Austria – the Minister has just mentioned it – was actually one of the first states and has already signed this agreement in October 2013. Overall, the agreement has been ratified by 43 countries, but 50 nations are needed for entry into force. What exactly is it about? – Contracting States must ensure that mercury is significantly reduced in industrial production. From 2020 on, it is forbidden to produce or sell mercury products – and I think that's right – such as certain bulbs or thermometers. In addition, mercury waste – which has already been mentioned – must only be stored under strict conditions and disposed of in the corresponding centres. The Convention is the first worldwide control instrument to limit the primary mining of mercury in the future, as well as to limit the production and trading of mercury products, such as batteries, electronic components, soaps, pesticides and measuring instruments. According to a report from the European Environment Agency, global combustion of coal and other fossil fuels is the main cause of mercury emissions. Other essential sources are metal and cement production, waste dumps, crematoria, as well as – this is also interesting – gold extraction, because mercury is also produced. It has also been said that mercury has a negative impact on our health, especially on the nervous system. I therefore think it is our obligation to ensure that this agreement is not only decided but actually implemented, in the sense of the population and, above all, in order to protect our children. Thank you.

As expected, it has become an energy debate or at least one over the eco electricity, there is a lot of confusion over it again and again. Planning security is important, which is why I expressly consider Mrs Weigerstorfer's initiative to be commendable. Unfortunately, the fact that this is devalued with these terms of the ÖVP – not by the Minister but by the honourable Member – is more often the rule here. You have to take that. Nevertheless, a few thoughts about the sequence: If the energy transition is to succeed, to a much greater extent and faster than now, then of course, it will need a mix of all energy sources. I want to come to the hydropower relatively quickly, because it is so controversial, but you have to look at it again and again in the individual case. The wind power is also controversial, I recognize it, but precisely the solar strategy, whether photovoltaic or the good old black loops for warming the water – a great thing! There is still so much potential, there is still so little use, but there is already a fight on the windwheels. - Okay, that's where we're more often on the operator's side, but it's true. But in the case of hydropower, it is overlooked that 80, 90, 95 percent of the last free-flow rivers in some federal states have either been installed, excavated, stowed or run-off, so that the river bed is only more visible, but nothing else; that is why it is not equivalent to me. Even in the sober economy there is the consideration of scarce goods, and freely flowing rivers have long become a scarce asset. It is therefore not equivalent, even if it appears economically there or there. [[Congratulation with the greens.]] But I want to go out to something else: With the interconnected group – recently I had a very good conversation there – and with the EStAG we have seen our wraps, as you know, but also those that are at least half the potential in the renunciation of the existing circumstances, which depends mainly on the turbines and other technical conditions. Many power plants are to be renewed in this sense anyway, so you can look so closely at where you are still in contact with the running water and where you are not. We will not be able to stand and conduct everything. The next generation also needs to be able to see water moving somewhere, and not some mud, because that will usually come from it. Now I come to a special problem in Graz. Some of you know what's going on. We could discuss whether or not we are standing there right now, but we still have an additional problem there. I've always believed that standing in the city is hot, but one thing is clear: The necessary accompanying buildings are extremely expensive especially in large cities. And if that is the case, then at least the electricity companies themselves should pay, but that is not the case! In Graz, the accompanying structures cost much more than the power plant itself to the Puntigam dam. You have to imagine that! We'll see what the slogan is: More private, less state! [[Abg. Kassinger: Who do they belong to...?]] The electricity companies will be subsidized with the Australian funds, which are also in it, and the € 100 to 200 million that costs the accompanying buildings, will be paid by the Stei-rische and Graz taxpayers – with subsidies from the country that go beyond the promotion of green electricity. There will be €7 million, €10.15 million from the urban water industry – that will keep everything quiet, that is totally opaque! And then the €7 million, because they are not allowed to go to the EStAG at all – or at least not to look like it – to the city of Graz, and the EStAG leaves the very small share of the costs in these accompanying buildings the same amount. It's a cleaner! This is carousel fraud with public money. That's completely opaque, and that's what it's all about! [[Congratulation with the greens.]] All of this is organised by our Federal Aid Authorities and the State Government, and that is why we – last sentence – do not come around this transparency and financial summit between all levels – federal, country, city. [[President Head gives the bell sign]] It cannot be that with € 100, 150 million we are promoting a power plant, which itself costs only € 80 million. This is completely absurd, and this unfavorable thing is turned away!
